People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. Well, honestly, it's... it's been a long time coming, I think. I guess the main reason I finally made the appointment is because of how things are going with my job. I'm a secondary school teacher, which I actually really enjoy—the kids are great, mostly, and being in the classroom is fine—but the behind-the-scenes stuff is just becoming this impossible mountain for me. 

Like, I will literally sit down at my dining room table with a massive stack of essays. I even have this whole routine where I make a cup of coffee, clear away all the clutter so I won't get distracted, get my grading pens out, and I sit down fully, one hundred percent intending to work. I’ll tell myself, 'Okay, you are going to grade just five of these before you get up.' But then... I don't know, maybe ten minutes will go by, and I’ll suddenly sort of snap to and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page the entire time. 

I’ll be looking right at the words, but I'm barely taking in what’s in front of me. Instead, I'm sitting there thinking about three completely unrelated things—like whether I remembered to reply to a parent's email from Tuesday, or what I need to pick up from the grocery store later, or some random documentary I watched over the weekend. And it happens with curriculum documents, too. I try to read through the state standards for the next unit, and my eyes just glaze over. It’s incredibly frustrating because I have the absolute best intentions to just get the work done, but I end up trapped there, staring at the paper, and an hour will pass with zero progress. It's just exhausting having to fight my own brain just to read a single essay.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it’s not... I wouldn't say it's anxiety. Like, I know exactly what you mean, because my sister actually gets really anxious about her job and she talks about this intense sense of dread she gets, like her chest gets tight and she can't breathe. But for me, it's not like that at all. I'm not sitting there worrying about failing or stressing over the quality of the students' work. 

It's more like... my brain just quietly slips out the back door while I'm not looking. I’m not sitting there agonizing over the essays, or stressing about parents being mad at me, or even worrying about general life stuff like money or whatever. I mean, sure, I'll think about the grocery store, like I mentioned before, but it's not a panicked, 'Oh no, I'm so worried I'll forget the milk!' kind of thought. It's just this totally neutral, completely unhelpful tangent. I'll just be sitting there thinking, 'Hmm, I wonder if they still have that specific brand of oat milk on sale, because last time they were out, and then I had to go to the other store down on 4th street, which is always an absolute nightmare for parking, especially on a Tuesday...' 

See? It's just this endless train of thought that goes on and on, completely peacefully, while the essay just sits there ignored. So, yeah, no racing heart or sweaty palms or that nervous pit in your stomach. Honestly, sometimes I'm almost too calm about it in the moment. I'm just totally zoned out, thinking about, I don't know, the history of the Roman Empire or how they manufacture glass, or whatever random rabbit hole my brain decided was infinitely more interesting than a 9th grader's thesis statement on To Kill a Mockingbird. 

It’s really only *afterward*, when I snap out of it and look at the clock and realize an entire hour has passed and I haven't capped my red pen once, that I get frustrated with myself. But in the actual moment when the distraction is happening? No, no worry or anxiety at all. Just... totally adrift.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it is... I wish it was just the grading, honestly. Because if it was just the paperwork, maybe I could just find a better system or force myself through it somehow. But no, it definitely bleeds into other areas, especially when I am supposed to be listening to people. It is actually becoming a pretty big issue, to be honest. 

Like, for example, we have these weekly planning meetings for the English department. They are usually on Wednesday afternoons right after the kids leave, so everyone is already a bit drained, but they are important because we have to align our lesson plans for the following week. And my department head will be going over something, like the new grading rubric for the district writing assessments. And I am sitting there, I am looking right at her, making solid eye contact, and I am even nodding at all the appropriate times. I look like the most engaged teacher in the room. But internally? I am completely gone. And the worst part, the absolute worst part, is when she suddenly stops and asks me what I think about adjusting the weighting on the grammar section, or something like that, and I just freeze. Because I realize I have absolutely no idea what she just said. I have completely missed the actual point of the last five minutes of her talking. 

And it happens at home, too, which is even harder to deal with. I will be sitting on the couch with my wife after dinner, just trying to unwind, and she will be telling me about her day or trying to figure out our weekend plans. And it is the exact same thing. I will be looking right at her, nodding along, giving her all the right physical cues that I am listening. But my brain has just... detached. It is like the audio is playing in the room, but my brain is not recording any of it. Then she will ask me which day I think works better for us to go to her parents house, and I just have to sit there and admit that I missed the entire point of what she was just saying. 

She gets so frustrated, and I really do not blame her at all, because from the outside it looks like I just do not care enough to listen. But I do care. I really want to hear about her day, and I want to be a good partner, but it is like my brain just refuses to stay anchored to the conversation. I will just drift off into thinking about the yard work or a podcast I listened to on the drive home, all while staring right at her. So, yeah, it is definitely not just the work tasks. It is everyday conversations, too, which really makes me feel terrible.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n3\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.9, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well, honestly, it's... it's been a long time coming, I think. I guess the main reason I finally made the appointment is because of how things are going with my job. I'm a secondary school teacher, which I actually really enjoy—the kids are great, mostly, and being in the classroom is fine—but the behind-the-scenes stuff is just becoming this impossible mountain for me. \n\nLike, I will literally sit down at my dining room table with a massive stack of essays. I even have this whole routine where I make a cup of coffee, clear away all the clutter so I won't get distracted, get my grading pens out, and I sit down fully, one hundred percent intending to work. I’ll tell myself, 'Okay, you are going to grade just five of these before you get up.' But then... I don't know, maybe ten minutes will go by, and I’ll suddenly sort of snap to and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page the entire time. \n\nI’ll be looking right at the words, but I'm barely taking in what’s in front of me. Instead, I'm sitting there thinking about three completely unrelated things—like whether I remembered to reply to a parent's email from Tuesday, or what I need to pick up from the grocery store later, or some random documentary I watched over the weekend. And it happens with curriculum documents, too. I try to read through the state standards for the next unit, and my eyes just glaze over. It’s incredibly frustrating because I have the absolute best intentions to just get the work done, but I end up trapped there, staring at the paper, and an hour will pass with zero progress. It's just exhausting having to fight my own brain just to read a single essay.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting there unable to focus on your work, do you notice that you're feeling worried or anxious about anything—either about the work itself, or about other things in general?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not... I wouldn't say it's anxiety. Like, I know exactly what you mean, because my sister actually gets really anxious about her job and she talks about this intense sense of dread she gets, like her chest gets tight and she can't breathe. But for me, it's not like that at all. I'm not sitting there worrying about failing or stressing over the quality of the students' work. \n\nIt's more like... my brain just quietly slips out the back door while I'm not looking. I’m not sitting there agonizing over the essays, or stressing about parents being mad at me, or even worrying about general life stuff like money or whatever. I mean, sure, I'll think about the grocery store, like I mentioned before, but it's not a panicked, 'Oh no, I'm so worried I'll forget the milk!' kind of thought. It's just this totally neutral, completely unhelpful tangent. I'll just be sitting there thinking, 'Hmm, I wonder if they still have that specific brand of oat milk on sale, because last time they were out, and then I had to go to the other store down on 4th street, which is always an absolute nightmare for parking, especially on a Tuesday...' \n\nSee? It's just this endless train of thought that goes on and on, completely peacefully, while the essay just sits there ignored. So, yeah, no racing heart or sweaty palms or that nervous pit in your stomach. Honestly, sometimes I'm almost too calm about it in the moment. I'm just totally zoned out, thinking about, I don't know, the history of the Roman Empire or how they manufacture glass, or whatever random rabbit hole my brain decided was infinitely more interesting than a 9th grader's thesis statement on To Kill a Mockingbird. \n\nIt’s really only *afterward*, when I snap out of it and look at the clock and realize an entire hour has passed and I haven't capped my red pen once, that I get frustrated with myself. But in the actual moment when the distraction is happening? No, no worry or anxiety at all. Just... totally adrift.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these kinds of concentration problems in other areas of your life—like when you're reading for pleasure, watching a movie, having a conversation with a friend, or doing hobbies you actually enjoy—or is it really just happening when you're trying to do work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is... I wish it was just the grading, honestly. Because if it was just the paperwork, maybe I could just find a better system or force myself through it somehow. But no, it definitely bleeds into other areas, especially when I am supposed to be listening to people. It is actually becoming a pretty big issue, to be honest. \n\nLike, for example, we have these weekly planning meetings for the English department. They are usually on Wednesday afternoons right after the kids leave, so everyone is already a bit drained, but they are important because we have to align our lesson plans for the following week. And my department head will be going over something, like the new grading rubric for the district writing assessments. And I am sitting there, I am looking right at her, making solid eye contact, and I am even nodding at all the appropriate times. I look like the most engaged teacher in the room. But internally? I am completely gone. And the worst part, the absolute worst part, is when she suddenly stops and asks me what I think about adjusting the weighting on the grammar section, or something like that, and I just freeze. Because I realize I have absolutely no idea what she just said. I have completely missed the actual point of the last five minutes of her talking. \n\nAnd it happens at home, too, which is even harder to deal with. I will be sitting on the couch with my wife after dinner, just trying to unwind, and she will be telling me about her day or trying to figure out our weekend plans. And it is the exact same thing. I will be looking right at her, nodding along, giving her all the right physical cues that I am listening. But my brain has just... detached. It is like the audio is playing in the room, but my brain is not recording any of it. Then she will ask me which day I think works better for us to go to her parents house, and I just have to sit there and admit that I missed the entire point of what she was just saying. \n\nShe gets so frustrated, and I really do not blame her at all, because from the outside it looks like I just do not care enough to listen. But I do care. I really want to hear about her day, and I want to be a good partner, but it is like my brain just refuses to stay anchored to the conversation. I will just drift off into thinking about the yard work or a podcast I listened to on the drive home, all while staring right at her. So, yeah, it is definitely not just the work tasks. It is everyday conversations, too, which really makes me feel terrible.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But for me, it's not like that at all. I'm not sitting there worrying about failing or stressing over the quality of the students' work. \n\nIt's more like... my brain just quietly slips out the back door while I'm not looking. I’m not sitting there agonizing over the essays, or stressing about parents being mad at me, or even worrying about general life stuff like money or whatever. I mean, sure, I'll think about the grocery store, like I mentioned before, but it's not a panicked, 'Oh no, I'm so worried I'll forget the milk!' kind of thought. It's just this totally neutral, completely unhelpful tangent. I'll just be sitting there thinking, 'Hmm, I wonder if they still have that specific brand of oat milk on sale, because last time they were out, and then I had to go to the other store down on 4th street, which is always an absolute nightmare for parking, especially on a Tuesday...' \n\nSee? It's just this endless train of thought that goes on and on, completely peacefully, while the essay just sits there ignored. So, yeah, no racing heart or sweaty palms or that nervous pit in your stomach. Honestly, sometimes I'm almost too calm about it in the moment. I'm just totally zoned out, thinking about, I don't know, the history of the Roman Empire or how they manufacture glass, or whatever random rabbit hole my brain decided was infinitely more interesting than a 9th grader's thesis statement on To Kill a Mockingbird. \n\nIt’s really only *afterward*, when I snap out of it and look at the clock and realize an entire hour has passed and I haven't capped my red pen once, that I get frustrated with myself. But in the actual moment when the distraction is happening? No, no worry or anxiety at all. Just... totally adrift.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these kinds of concentration problems in other areas of your life—like when you're reading for pleasure, watching a movie, having a conversation with a friend, or doing hobbies you actually enjoy—or is it really just happening when you're trying to do work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is... I wish it was just the grading, honestly. Because if it was just the paperwork, maybe I could just find a better system or force myself through it somehow. But no, it definitely bleeds into other areas, especially when I am supposed to be listening to people. It is actually becoming a pretty big issue, to be honest. \n\nLike, for example, we have these weekly planning meetings for the English department. They are usually on Wednesday afternoons right after the kids leave, so everyone is already a bit drained, but they are important because we have to align our lesson plans for the following week. And my department head will be going over something, like the new grading rubric for the district writing assessments. And I am sitting there, I am looking right at her, making solid eye contact, and I am even nodding at all the appropriate times. I look like the most engaged teacher in the room. But internally? I am completely gone. And the worst part, the absolute worst part, is when she suddenly stops and asks me what I think about adjusting the weighting on the grammar section, or something like that, and I just freeze. Because I realize I have absolutely no idea what she just said. I have completely missed the actual point of the last five minutes of her talking. \n\nAnd it happens at home, too, which is even harder to deal with. I will be sitting on the couch with my wife after dinner, just trying to unwind, and she will be telling me about her day or trying to figure out our weekend plans. And it is the exact same thing. I will be looking right at her, nodding along, giving her all the right physical cues that I am listening. But my brain has just... detached. It is like the audio is playing in the room, but my brain is not recording any of it. Then she will ask me which day I think works better for us to go to her parents house, and I just have to sit there and admit that I missed the entire point of what she was just saying. \n\nShe gets so frustrated, and I really do not blame her at all, because from the outside it looks like I just do not care enough to listen. But I do care. I really want to hear about her day, and I want to be a good partner, but it is like my brain just refuses to stay anchored to the conversation. I will just drift off into thinking about the yard work or a podcast I listened to on the drive home, all while staring right at her. So, yeah, it is definitely not just the work tasks. It is everyday conversations, too, which really makes me feel terrible.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been experiencing these concentration and attention problems? Did they start suddenly at some point, or have they been something you've dealt with for most of your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Even in primary school, my report cards said things like “bright but careless” and “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised,” and my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled in my backpack that I had forgotten to turn in.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
You know, it's funny you ask that, because for a long time I really thought this was just a recent thing. I kept telling myself that I was just burned out from teaching, or that the curriculum changes were just getting to me, but... well, when I actually sat down and really thought about it, and especially after talking to my mom a while back, I realized this has basically been my normal for my entire life. It definitely wasn't sudden at all. 

I mean, going all the way back to primary school, it was happening. My mom is one of those people who keeps absolutely everything, so she has this massive plastic tub in their attic with all my childhood stuff, and we were actually looking through my old report cards not that long ago. And it was honestly kind of wild to read them now, with the perspective I have as a teacher myself, because every single year, from like first grade all the way up, my teachers were writing the exact same comments. Over and over, the remarks were always things like, “bright but careless,” or they’d write that I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” Which, you know, is exactly what I was just saying about needing to force myself to sit at the dining room table to grade, right? 

And it wasn't just the report cards, either. I distinctly remember the absolute state of my backpack back then. It was a complete disaster zone. My parents would make me sit at the kitchen table on Friday afternoons and they would just dump the whole bag out upside down to clean it, and they were always, without fail, finding all these worksheets just shoved at the bottom, completely crumpled up. And the most frustrating part—for them and for me—was that a lot of the time, I had actually done the work! I had sat there and done the math problems or the spelling words, but I just completely forgot to actually take the paper out of my bag and put it in the tray to turn it in. 

My dad would get so exasperated, just sitting there trying to smooth out these incredibly wrinkled, destroyed pieces of paper so I could hand them in late. So my grades would suffer not because I didn't understand the lessons, but because there was this total disconnect between doing the task and actually finishing the final step. So, yeah, to answer your question, it’s definitely not a new development. It has always been there. I think the only difference now is that I don't have my parents or a teacher standing over my shoulder making sure I empty my backpack or finish my work. I'm the adult now, so all that structure is gone, and I'm just left dealing with the exact same brain I had when I was seven.
=====================================

You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S002\n  name: Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty sustaining attention in tasks or play activities.\n  manifestation: I came in because I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document fully intending to work, and within ten minutes I realize I’ve been staring at the same page, thinking about three other things, and barely taking in what’s in front of me.\n- key: S003\n  name: Listening_Difficulty\n  description: Often does not seem to listen when spoken to directly.\n  manifestation: In planning meetings or even at home with my wife, I’ll be looking right at the person and nodding, but then they ask what I think and I’ve missed the actual point of what they just said.\n- key: S004\n  name: Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty\n  description: Often does not follow through on instructions and fails to finish schoolwork, chores, or duties in the workplace.\n  manifestation: If my department chair gives me three or four things to do after a meeting, I usually start the first one, maybe even do most of it, but then the last step—sending the file, updating the shared calendar, printing the handouts—just doesn’t happen unless someone reminds me.\n- key: S007\n  name: Losing_Items\n  description: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n  manifestation: I’m constantly hunting for things I need for the day, like my classroom keys, lesson notes, USB drive, or the whistle and cards for refereeing, even when I’m sure I had them in my hand five minutes earlier.\n- key: S009\n  name: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\n  description: Often forgetful in daily activities (e.g., doing chores, running errands; for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I forget ordinary responsibilities more than I want to admit, like replying to a parent email, paying a bill before the late notice comes, or picking up something my wife asked for on the way home.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in primary school, my report cards said things like “bright but careless” and “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised,” and my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled in my backpack that I had forgotten to turn in.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work, the missed follow-through has become serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan, and at home my wife has started taking over bills and reminders because she can’t count on me to keep track of them.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably worse for about 11 months, since the school changed the curriculum calendar and added the after-school planning meetings.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S002\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you're sitting there unable to focus on your work, do you notice that you're feeling worried or anxious about anything—either about the work itself, or about other things in general?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not... I wouldn't say it's anxiety. Like, I know exactly what you mean, because my sister actually gets really anxious about her job and she talks about this intense sense of dread she gets, like her chest gets tight and she can't breathe. But for me, it's not like that at all. I'm not sitting there worrying about failing or stressing over the quality of the students' work. \n\nIt's more like... my brain just quietly slips out the back door while I'm not looking. I’m not sitting there agonizing over the essays, or stressing about parents being mad at me, or even worrying about general life stuff like money or whatever. I mean, sure, I'll think about the grocery store, like I mentioned before, but it's not a panicked, 'Oh no, I'm so worried I'll forget the milk!' kind of thought. It's just this totally neutral, completely unhelpful tangent. I'll just be sitting there thinking, 'Hmm, I wonder if they still have that specific brand of oat milk on sale, because last time they were out, and then I had to go to the other store down on 4th street, which is always an absolute nightmare for parking, especially on a Tuesday...' \n\nSee? It's just this endless train of thought that goes on and on, completely peacefully, while the essay just sits there ignored. So, yeah, no racing heart or sweaty palms or that nervous pit in your stomach. Honestly, sometimes I'm almost too calm about it in the moment. I'm just totally zoned out, thinking about, I don't know, the history of the Roman Empire or how they manufacture glass, or whatever random rabbit hole my brain decided was infinitely more interesting than a 9th grader's thesis statement on To Kill a Mockingbird. \n\nIt’s really only *afterward*, when I snap out of it and look at the clock and realize an entire hour has passed and I haven't capped my red pen once, that I get frustrated with myself. But in the actual moment when the distraction is happening? No, no worry or anxiety at all. Just... totally adrift.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these kinds of concentration problems in other areas of your life—like when you're reading for pleasure, watching a movie, having a conversation with a friend, or doing hobbies you actually enjoy—or is it really just happening when you're trying to do work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is... I wish it was just the grading, honestly. Because if it was just the paperwork, maybe I could just find a better system or force myself through it somehow. But no, it definitely bleeds into other areas, especially when I am supposed to be listening to people. It is actually becoming a pretty big issue, to be honest. \n\nLike, for example, we have these weekly planning meetings for the English department. They are usually on Wednesday afternoons right after the kids leave, so everyone is already a bit drained, but they are important because we have to align our lesson plans for the following week. And my department head will be going over something, like the new grading rubric for the district writing assessments. And I am sitting there, I am looking right at her, making solid eye contact, and I am even nodding at all the appropriate times. I look like the most engaged teacher in the room. But internally? I am completely gone. And the worst part, the absolute worst part, is when she suddenly stops and asks me what I think about adjusting the weighting on the grammar section, or something like that, and I just freeze. Because I realize I have absolutely no idea what she just said. I have completely missed the actual point of the last five minutes of her talking. \n\nAnd it happens at home, too, which is even harder to deal with. I will be sitting on the couch with my wife after dinner, just trying to unwind, and she will be telling me about her day or trying to figure out our weekend plans. And it is the exact same thing. I will be looking right at her, nodding along, giving her all the right physical cues that I am listening. But my brain has just... detached. It is like the audio is playing in the room, but my brain is not recording any of it. Then she will ask me which day I think works better for us to go to her parents house, and I just have to sit there and admit that I missed the entire point of what she was just saying. \n\nShe gets so frustrated, and I really do not blame her at all, because from the outside it looks like I just do not care enough to listen. But I do care. I really want to hear about her day, and I want to be a good partner, but it is like my brain just refuses to stay anchored to the conversation. I will just drift off into thinking about the yard work or a podcast I listened to on the drive home, all while staring right at her. So, yeah, it is definitely not just the work tasks. It is everyday conversations, too, which really makes me feel terrible.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these concentration and attention problems? Did they start suddenly at some point, or have they been something you've dealt with for most of your life?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because for a long time I really thought this was just a recent thing. I kept telling myself that I was just burned out from teaching, or that the curriculum changes were just getting to me, but... well, when I actually sat down and really thought about it, and especially after talking to my mom a while back, I realized this has basically been my normal for my entire life. It definitely wasn't sudden at all. \n\nI mean, going all the way back to primary school, it was happening. My mom is one of those people who keeps absolutely everything, so she has this massive plastic tub in their attic with all my childhood stuff, and we were actually looking through my old report cards not that long ago. And it was honestly kind of wild to read them now, with the perspective I have as a teacher myself, because every single year, from like first grade all the way up, my teachers were writing the exact same comments. Over and over, the remarks were always things like, “bright but careless,” or they’d write that I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” Which, you know, is exactly what I was just saying about needing to force myself to sit at the dining room table to grade, right? \n\nAnd it wasn't just the report cards, either. I distinctly remember the absolute state of my backpack back then. It was a complete disaster zone. My parents would make me sit at the kitchen table on Friday afternoons and they would just dump the whole bag out upside down to clean it, and they were always, without fail, finding all these worksheets just shoved at the bottom, completely crumpled up. And the most frustrating part—for them and for me—was that a lot of the time, I had actually done the work! I had sat there and done the math problems or the spelling words, but I just completely forgot to actually take the paper out of my bag and put it in the tray to turn it in. \n\nMy dad would get so exasperated, just sitting there trying to smooth out these incredibly wrinkled, destroyed pieces of paper so I could hand them in late. So my grades would suffer not because I didn't understand the lessons, but because there was this total disconnect between doing the task and actually finishing the final step. So, yeah, to answer your question, it’s definitely not a new development. It has always been there. I think the only difference now is that I don't have my parents or a teacher standing over my shoulder making sure I empty my backpack or finish my work. I'm the adult now, so all that structure is gone, and I'm just left dealing with the exact same brain I had when I was seven.\n\n[Last Question]\nGrowing up and even now, have you noticed that you tend to be restless or fidgety—like needing to move around a lot, or feeling like you have excess energy that's hard to contain? Or do you find yourself acting on impulses without thinking things through first?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, I see that kind of thing all the time with my students, obviously. You get a classroom full of fourteen-year-olds and it is just a constant, never-ending symphony of tapping pencils and bouncing knees and kids asking to go to the bathroom just so they have an excuse to walk down the hall. I have this one kid in my third period, actually, who literally cannot keep all four legs of his chair on the floor for more than three minutes at a time. It is like he is physically vibrating with all this pent-up energy, just constantly shifting and moving. 

But for me? No, I don't get that feeling at all. I am actually the complete opposite. If anything, I can sit entirely too still. Like I was saying before about zoning out when I'm supposed to be grading or when I'm on the couch... I can sit in the exact same position for an hour or two without moving a muscle, just completely lost in whatever random thought process my brain has wandered off into. I definitely don't feel that physical urge to get up and pace around or burn off energy. Honestly, by the time the final bell rings at school, I don't feel like I have any excess energy to burn anyway. I usually just want to sink into a chair and not move for the rest of the evening. 

And as for the impulsivity part of your question... no, I wouldn't say that fits me either. I am definitely not the kind of person to just jump into things without thinking or make rash decisions on the fly. If anything, I probably overthink things, or I just don't take action at all because I get stuck in my own head trying to figure out the absolute best way to do something, and then I just end up doing nothing. 

Like, just as an example, when we were trying to pick a color to paint the guest room last summer, I spent three entire weeks looking at paint swatches and reading online reviews of different brands of eggshell finishes, just completely going down this rabbit hole of interior design blogs, and I never actually bought the paint. My wife finally just went to the hardware store and picked a color herself because I was so paralyzed by thinking about it too much. So, yeah, I am not blurting things out or acting without thinking. It is really just the focus and the spacing out that is the problem for me, not any kind of physical restlessness or impulsive behavior.

Then she will ask me which day I think works better for us to go to her parents house, and I just have to sit there and admit that I missed the entire point of what she was just saying. \n\nShe gets so frustrated, and I really do not blame her at all, because from the outside it looks like I just do not care enough to listen. But I do care. I really want to hear about her day, and I want to be a good partner, but it is like my brain just refuses to stay anchored to the conversation. I will just drift off into thinking about the yard work or a podcast I listened to on the drive home, all while staring right at her. So, yeah, it is definitely not just the work tasks. It is everyday conversations, too, which really makes me feel terrible.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these concentration and attention problems? Did they start suddenly at some point, or have they been something you've dealt with for most of your life?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because for a long time I really thought this was just a recent thing. I kept telling myself that I was just burned out from teaching, or that the curriculum changes were just getting to me, but... well, when I actually sat down and really thought about it, and especially after talking to my mom a while back, I realized this has basically been my normal for my entire life. It definitely wasn't sudden at all. \n\nI mean, going all the way back to primary school, it was happening. My mom is one of those people who keeps absolutely everything, so she has this massive plastic tub in their attic with all my childhood stuff, and we were actually looking through my old report cards not that long ago. And it was honestly kind of wild to read them now, with the perspective I have as a teacher myself, because every single year, from like first grade all the way up, my teachers were writing the exact same comments. Over and over, the remarks were always things like, “bright but careless,” or they’d write that I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” Which, you know, is exactly what I was just saying about needing to force myself to sit at the dining room table to grade, right? \n\nAnd it wasn't just the report cards, either. I distinctly remember the absolute state of my backpack back then. It was a complete disaster zone. My parents would make me sit at the kitchen table on Friday afternoons and they would just dump the whole bag out upside down to clean it, and they were always, without fail, finding all these worksheets just shoved at the bottom, completely crumpled up. And the most frustrating part—for them and for me—was that a lot of the time, I had actually done the work! I had sat there and done the math problems or the spelling words, but I just completely forgot to actually take the paper out of my bag and put it in the tray to turn it in. \n\nMy dad would get so exasperated, just sitting there trying to smooth out these incredibly wrinkled, destroyed pieces of paper so I could hand them in late. So my grades would suffer not because I didn't understand the lessons, but because there was this total disconnect between doing the task and actually finishing the final step. So, yeah, to answer your question, it’s definitely not a new development. It has always been there. I think the only difference now is that I don't have my parents or a teacher standing over my shoulder making sure I empty my backpack or finish my work. I'm the adult now, so all that structure is gone, and I'm just left dealing with the exact same brain I had when I was seven.\n\nDoctor: Growing up and even now, have you noticed that you tend to be restless or fidgety—like needing to move around a lot, or feeling like you have excess energy that's hard to contain? Or do you find yourself acting on impulses without thinking things through first?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I see that kind of thing all the time with my students, obviously. You get a classroom full of fourteen-year-olds and it is just a constant, never-ending symphony of tapping pencils and bouncing knees and kids asking to go to the bathroom just so they have an excuse to walk down the hall. I have this one kid in my third period, actually, who literally cannot keep all four legs of his chair on the floor for more than three minutes at a time. It is like he is physically vibrating with all this pent-up energy, just constantly shifting and moving. \n\nBut for me? No, I don't get that feeling at all. I am actually the complete opposite. If anything, I can sit entirely too still. Like I was saying before about zoning out when I'm supposed to be grading or when I'm on the couch... I can sit in the exact same position for an hour or two without moving a muscle, just completely lost in whatever random thought process my brain has wandered off into. I definitely don't feel that physical urge to get up and pace around or burn off energy. Honestly, by the time the final bell rings at school, I don't feel like I have any excess energy to burn anyway. I usually just want to sink into a chair and not move for the rest of the evening. \n\nAnd as for the impulsivity part of your question... no, I wouldn't say that fits me either. I am definitely not the kind of person to just jump into things without thinking or make rash decisions on the fly. If anything, I probably overthink things, or I just don't take action at all because I get stuck in my own head trying to figure out the absolute best way to do something, and then I just end up doing nothing. \n\nLike, just as an example, when we were trying to pick a color to paint the guest room last summer, I spent three entire weeks looking at paint swatches and reading online reviews of different brands of eggshell finishes, just completely going down this rabbit hole of interior design blogs, and I never actually bought the paint. My wife finally just went to the hardware store and picked a color herself because I was so paralyzed by thinking about it too much. So, yeah, I am not blurting things out or acting without thinking. It is really just the focus and the spacing out that is the problem for me, not any kind of physical restlessness or impulsive behavior.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Lifelong history of inattention, difficulty with task completion, organizational problems, and poor sustained attention in multiple contexts (work, conversations, hobbies) strongly supports ADHD predominantly inattentive presentation. No anxiety, no hyperactivity symptoms.",
  "is_final": false

Did they start suddenly at some point, or have they been something you've dealt with for most of your life?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because for a long time I really thought this was just a recent thing. I kept telling myself that I was just burned out from teaching, or that the curriculum changes were just getting to me, but... well, when I actually sat down and really thought about it, and especially after talking to my mom a while back, I realized this has basically been my normal for my entire life. It definitely wasn't sudden at all. \n\nI mean, going all the way back to primary school, it was happening. My mom is one of those people who keeps absolutely everything, so she has this massive plastic tub in their attic with all my childhood stuff, and we were actually looking through my old report cards not that long ago. And it was honestly kind of wild to read them now, with the perspective I have as a teacher myself, because every single year, from like first grade all the way up, my teachers were writing the exact same comments. Over and over, the remarks were always things like, “bright but careless,” or they’d write that I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” Which, you know, is exactly what I was just saying about needing to force myself to sit at the dining room table to grade, right? \n\nAnd it wasn't just the report cards, either. I distinctly remember the absolute state of my backpack back then. It was a complete disaster zone. My parents would make me sit at the kitchen table on Friday afternoons and they would just dump the whole bag out upside down to clean it, and they were always, without fail, finding all these worksheets just shoved at the bottom, completely crumpled up. And the most frustrating part—for them and for me—was that a lot of the time, I had actually done the work! I had sat there and done the math problems or the spelling words, but I just completely forgot to actually take the paper out of my bag and put it in the tray to turn it in. \n\nMy dad would get so exasperated, just sitting there trying to smooth out these incredibly wrinkled, destroyed pieces of paper so I could hand them in late. So my grades would suffer not because I didn't understand the lessons, but because there was this total disconnect between doing the task and actually finishing the final step. So, yeah, to answer your question, it’s definitely not a new development. It has always been there. I think the only difference now is that I don't have my parents or a teacher standing over my shoulder making sure I empty my backpack or finish my work. I'm the adult now, so all that structure is gone, and I'm just left dealing with the exact same brain I had when I was seven.\n\nDoctor: Growing up and even now, have you noticed that you tend to be restless or fidgety—like needing to move around a lot, or feeling like you have excess energy that's hard to contain? Or do you find yourself acting on impulses without thinking things through first?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I see that kind of thing all the time with my students, obviously. You get a classroom full of fourteen-year-olds and it is just a constant, never-ending symphony of tapping pencils and bouncing knees and kids asking to go to the bathroom just so they have an excuse to walk down the hall. I have this one kid in my third period, actually, who literally cannot keep all four legs of his chair on the floor for more than three minutes at a time. It is like he is physically vibrating with all this pent-up energy, just constantly shifting and moving. \n\nBut for me? No, I don't get that feeling at all. I am actually the complete opposite. If anything, I can sit entirely too still. Like I was saying before about zoning out when I'm supposed to be grading or when I'm on the couch... I can sit in the exact same position for an hour or two without moving a muscle, just completely lost in whatever random thought process my brain has wandered off into. I definitely don't feel that physical urge to get up and pace around or burn off energy. Honestly, by the time the final bell rings at school, I don't feel like I have any excess energy to burn anyway. I usually just want to sink into a chair and not move for the rest of the evening. \n\nAnd as for the impulsivity part of your question... no, I wouldn't say that fits me either. I am definitely not the kind of person to just jump into things without thinking or make rash decisions on the fly. If anything, I probably overthink things, or I just don't take action at all because I get stuck in my own head trying to figure out the absolute best way to do something, and then I just end up doing nothing. \n\nLike, just as an example, when we were trying to pick a color to paint the guest room last summer, I spent three entire weeks looking at paint swatches and reading online reviews of different brands of eggshell finishes, just completely going down this rabbit hole of interior design blogs, and I never actually bought the paint. My wife finally just went to the hardware store and picked a color herself because I was so paralyzed by thinking about it too much. So, yeah, I am not blurting things out or acting without thinking. It is really just the focus and the spacing out that is the problem for me, not any kind of physical restlessness or impulsive behavior.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you were a child—say, in elementary school or middle school—did your teachers or parents ever describe you as hyperactive, overly talkative, or someone who had trouble sitting still in class or at home? Or did they ever mention that you acted without thinking or interrupted people a lot?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. No, honestly, not at all. It kind of goes back to what I was just saying about those old report cards my mom kept in that giant plastic tub in the attic. Because, you know, as a teacher now, I know exactly the kind of kid you're talking about. The ones who blurt out answers before you even finish the question, or the ones who are constantly up sharpening their pencil five times a period, or just physically cannot stop talking to the person next to them... but that was never me. Not even a little bit. 

If anything, I think my teachers probably liked having me in class because I was so quiet. I was never a behavior problem. I never interrupted anyone because, well, frankly, I was usually too lost in my own little world to even know what the conversation was about in the first place, let alone interrupt it. I was the kid staring out the window at the clouds, or... or drawing these little elaborate mazes in the margins of my notebook while the teacher was talking. 

I actually remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, she told my parents at parent-teacher conferences that I was a sweet boy, but a million miles away. That was the exact phrase she used, and my mom used to repeat it all the time. So, no, nobody ever called me hyperactive or overly talkative. I was basically just a piece of furniture in the classroom, you know? Just quietly sitting there at my desk, completely zoned out, definitely not bothering anyone, but also not really absorbing anything either. So yeah, the physical restlessness and the blurting things out, that just wasn't me back then, just like it isn't me now.

But for me, it's not like that at all. I’m not sitting there agonizing over the essays, or stressing about parents being mad at me, or even worrying about general life stuff like money or whatever. I mean, sure, I'll think about the grocery store, like I mentioned before, but it's not a panicked, 'Oh no, I'm so worried I'll forget the milk!' kind of thought. It's just this totally neutral, completely unhelpful tangent. I'll just be sitting there thinking, 'Hmm, I wonder if they still have that specific brand of oat milk on sale, because last time they were out, and then I had to go to the other store down on 4th street, which is always an absolute nightmare for parking, especially on a Tuesday...' \n\nSee? It's just this endless train of thought that goes on and on, completely peacefully, while the essay just sits there ignored. So, yeah, no racing heart or sweaty palms or that nervous pit in your stomach. Honestly, sometimes I'm almost too calm about it in the moment. I'm just totally zoned out, thinking about, I don't know, the history of the Roman Empire or how they manufacture glass, or whatever random rabbit hole my brain decided was infinitely more interesting than a 9th grader's thesis statement on To Kill a Mockingbird. \n\nIt’s really only *afterward*, when I snap out of it and look at the clock and realize an entire hour has passed and I haven't capped my red pen once, that I get frustrated with myself. But in the actual moment when the distraction is happening? No, no worry or anxiety at all. Just... totally adrift.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these kinds of concentration problems in other areas of your life—like when you're reading for pleasure, watching a movie, having a conversation with a friend, or doing hobbies you actually enjoy—or is it really just happening when you're trying to do work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is... I wish it was just the grading, honestly. Because if it was just the paperwork, maybe I could just find a better system or force myself through it somehow. But no, it definitely bleeds into other areas, especially when I am supposed to be listening to people. It is actually becoming a pretty big issue, to be honest. \n\nLike, for example, we have these weekly planning meetings for the English department. They are usually on Wednesday afternoons right after the kids leave, so everyone is already a bit drained, but they are important because we have to align our lesson plans for the following week. And my department head will be going over something, like the new grading rubric for the district writing assessments. And I am sitting there, I am looking right at her, making solid eye contact, and I am even nodding at all the appropriate times. I look like the most engaged teacher in the room. But internally? I am completely gone. And the worst part, the absolute worst part, is when she suddenly stops and asks me what I think about adjusting the weighting on the grammar section, or something like that, and I just freeze. Because I realize I have absolutely no idea what she just said. I have completely missed the actual point of the last five minutes of her talking. \n\nAnd it happens at home, too, which is even harder to deal with. I will be sitting on the couch with my wife after dinner, just trying to unwind, and she will be telling me about her day or trying to figure out our weekend plans. And it is the exact same thing. I will be looking right at her, nodding along, giving her all the right physical cues that I am listening. But my brain has just... detached. It is like the audio is playing in the room, but my brain is not recording any of it. Then she will ask me which day I think works better for us to go to her parents house, and I just have to sit there and admit that I missed the entire point of what she was just saying. \n\nShe gets so frustrated, and I really do not blame her at all, because from the outside it looks like I just do not care enough to listen. But I do care. I really want to hear about her day, and I want to be a good partner, but it is like my brain just refuses to stay anchored to the conversation. I will just drift off into thinking about the yard work or a podcast I listened to on the drive home, all while staring right at her. So, yeah, it is definitely not just the work tasks. It is everyday conversations, too, which really makes me feel terrible.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these concentration and attention problems? Did they start suddenly at some point, or have they been something you've dealt with for most of your life?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because for a long time I really thought this was just a recent thing. I kept telling myself that I was just burned out from teaching, or that the curriculum changes were just getting to me, but... well, when I actually sat down and really thought about it, and especially after talking to my mom a while back, I realized this has basically been my normal for my entire life. It definitely wasn't sudden at all. \n\nI mean, going all the way back to primary school, it was happening. My mom is one of those people who keeps absolutely everything, so she has this massive plastic tub in their attic with all my childhood stuff, and we were actually looking through my old report cards not that long ago. And it was honestly kind of wild to read them now, with the perspective I have as a teacher myself, because every single year, from like first grade all the way up, my teachers were writing the exact same comments. Over and over, the remarks were always things like, “bright but careless,” or they’d write that I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” Which, you know, is exactly what I was just saying about needing to force myself to sit at the dining room table to grade, right? \n\nAnd it wasn't just the report cards, either. I distinctly remember the absolute state of my backpack back then. It was a complete disaster zone. My parents would make me sit at the kitchen table on Friday afternoons and they would just dump the whole bag out upside down to clean it, and they were always, without fail, finding all these worksheets just shoved at the bottom, completely crumpled up. And the most frustrating part—for them and for me—was that a lot of the time, I had actually done the work! I had sat there and done the math problems or the spelling words, but I just completely forgot to actually take the paper out of my bag and put it in the tray to turn it in. \n\nMy dad would get so exasperated, just sitting there trying to smooth out these incredibly wrinkled, destroyed pieces of paper so I could hand them in late. So my grades would suffer not because I didn't understand the lessons, but because there was this total disconnect between doing the task and actually finishing the final step. So, yeah, to answer your question, it’s definitely not a new development. It has always been there. I think the only difference now is that I don't have my parents or a teacher standing over my shoulder making sure I empty my backpack or finish my work. I'm the adult now, so all that structure is gone, and I'm just left dealing with the exact same brain I had when I was seven.\n\nDoctor: Growing up and even now, have you noticed that you tend to be restless or fidgety—like needing to move around a lot, or feeling like you have excess energy that's hard to contain? Or do you find yourself acting on impulses without thinking things through first?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I see that kind of thing all the time with my students, obviously. You get a classroom full of fourteen-year-olds and it is just a constant, never-ending symphony of tapping pencils and bouncing knees and kids asking to go to the bathroom just so they have an excuse to walk down the hall. I have this one kid in my third period, actually, who literally cannot keep all four legs of his chair on the floor for more than three minutes at a time. It is like he is physically vibrating with all this pent-up energy, just constantly shifting and moving. \n\nBut for me? No, I don't get that feeling at all. I am actually the complete opposite. If anything, I can sit entirely too still. Like I was saying before about zoning out when I'm supposed to be grading or when I'm on the couch... I can sit in the exact same position for an hour or two without moving a muscle, just completely lost in whatever random thought process my brain has wandered off into. I definitely don't feel that physical urge to get up and pace around or burn off energy. Honestly, by the time the final bell rings at school, I don't feel like I have any excess energy to burn anyway. I usually just want to sink into a chair and not move for the rest of the evening. \n\nAnd as for the impulsivity part of your question... no, I wouldn't say that fits me either. I am definitely not the kind of person to just jump into things without thinking or make rash decisions on the fly. If anything, I probably overthink things, or I just don't take action at all because I get stuck in my own head trying to figure out the absolute best way to do something, and then I just end up doing nothing. \n\nLike, just as an example, when we were trying to pick a color to paint the guest room last summer, I spent three entire weeks looking at paint swatches and reading online reviews of different brands of eggshell finishes, just completely going down this rabbit hole of interior design blogs, and I never actually bought the paint. My wife finally just went to the hardware store and picked a color herself because I was so paralyzed by thinking about it too much. So, yeah, I am not blurting things out or acting without thinking. It is really just the focus and the spacing out that is the problem for me, not any kind of physical restlessness or impulsive behavior.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child—say, in elementary school or middle school—did your teachers or parents ever describe you as hyperactive, overly talkative, or someone who had trouble sitting still in class or at home? Or did they ever mention that you acted without thinking or interrupted people a lot?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not at all. It kind of goes back to what I was just saying about those old report cards my mom kept in that giant plastic tub in the attic. Because, you know, as a teacher now, I know exactly the kind of kid you're talking about. The ones who blurt out answers before you even finish the question, or the ones who are constantly up sharpening their pencil five times a period, or just physically cannot stop talking to the person next to them... but that was never me. Not even a little bit. \n\nIf anything, I think my teachers probably liked having me in class because I was so quiet. I was never a behavior problem. I never interrupted anyone because, well, frankly, I was usually too lost in my own little world to even know what the conversation was about in the first place, let alone interrupt it. I was the kid staring out the window at the clouds, or... or drawing these little elaborate mazes in the margins of my notebook while the teacher was talking. \n\nI actually remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, she told my parents at parent-teacher conferences that I was a sweet boy, but a million miles away. That was the exact phrase she used, and my mom used to repeat it all the time. So, no, nobody ever called me hyperactive or overly talkative. I was basically just a piece of furniture in the classroom, you know? Just quietly sitting there at my desk, completely zoned out, definitely not bothering anyone, but also not really absorbing anything either. So yeah, the physical restlessness and the blurting things out, that just wasn't me back then, just like it isn't me now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

So yeah, the physical restlessness and the blurting things out, that just wasn't me back then, just like it isn't me now.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever been formally evaluated or diagnosed with ADHD—attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder—either as a child or as an adult?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, actually. Never. Not once. Which, I mean, looking back on it now, considering everything we just talked about with the report cards and the absolute disaster zone of my backpack, you would really think someone would have flagged it, right? But no, I never saw a doctor or a school psychologist or anyone like that when I was a kid. 

I think... well, honestly, I really think it just comes down to the fact that I wasn't causing any trouble for anyone but myself. Back then—I was in elementary school in the late nineties, early two-thousands—and I feel like the only kids who ever got sent to the counselor's office or got evaluated for anything were the ones who were literally bouncing off the walls. The ones throwing erasers or refusing to stay in their seats, like the kids I was just talking about. Since I was just quietly sitting there drawing my little mazes or staring at the ceiling, I just completely flew under the radar. 

My parents, you know, they meant well, but they just thought I needed to... well, apply myself more. That was the big phrase in our house. "He's smart, he just needs to apply himself." They figured I was maybe a little lazy, definitely disorganized, but they never once thought it was a clinical thing that needed an actual official evaluation. They just thought I'd eventually grow out of it if they made me sit at the kitchen table and clean my folders out enough times. 

And then as an adult... I mean, you just kind of get used to your own brain, don't you? You just assume everyone else is struggling just as hard to get through their paperwork or pay attention in long staff meetings, and you figure they're just somehow better at hiding it or pushing through it than you are. I never even considered getting formally checked out for anything until very recently. It wasn't until the grading just started piling up so badly and my wife sort of gently suggested that maybe this wasn't just normal teacher burnout, that I even thought about talking to somebody. So, yeah, this right here, talking to you, is actually the very first time I've ever sat down with a professional to look into this. It's all completely new territory for me.
