My mom is one of those people who keeps absolutely everything, so she has this massive plastic tub in their attic with all my childhood stuff, and we were actually looking through my old report cards not that long ago. And it was honestly kind of wild to read them now, with the perspective I have as a teacher myself, because every single year, from like first grade all the way up, my teachers were writing the exact same comments. Over and over, the remarks were always things like, “bright but careless,” or they’d write that I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” Which, you know, is exactly what I was just saying about needing to force myself to sit at the dining room table to grade, right? \n\nAnd it wasn't just the report cards, either. I distinctly remember the absolute state of my backpack back then. It was a complete disaster zone. My parents would make me sit at the kitchen table on Friday afternoons and they would just dump the whole bag out upside down to clean it, and they were always, without fail, finding all these worksheets just shoved at the bottom, completely crumpled up. And the most frustrating part—for them and for me—was that a lot of the time, I had actually done the work! I had sat there and done the math problems or the spelling words, but I just completely forgot to actually take the paper out of my bag and put it in the tray to turn it in. \n\nMy dad would get so exasperated, just sitting there trying to smooth out these incredibly wrinkled, destroyed pieces of paper so I could hand them in late. So my grades would suffer not because I didn't understand the lessons, but because there was this total disconnect between doing the task and actually finishing the final step. So, yeah, to answer your question, it’s definitely not a new development. It has always been there. I think the only difference now is that I don't have my parents or a teacher standing over my shoulder making sure I empty my backpack or finish my work. I'm the adult now, so all that structure is gone, and I'm just left dealing with the exact same brain I had when I was seven.\n\nDoctor: Growing up and even now, have you noticed that you tend to be restless or fidgety—like needing to move around a lot, or feeling like you have excess energy that's hard to contain? Or do you find yourself acting on impulses without thinking things through first?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I see that kind of thing all the time with my students, obviously. You get a classroom full of fourteen-year-olds and it is just a constant, never-ending symphony of tapping pencils and bouncing knees and kids asking to go to the bathroom just so they have an excuse to walk down the hall. I have this one kid in my third period, actually, who literally cannot keep all four legs of his chair on the floor for more than three minutes at a time. It is like he is physically vibrating with all this pent-up energy, just constantly shifting and moving. \n\nBut for me? No, I don't get that feeling at all. I am actually the complete opposite. If anything, I can sit entirely too still. Like I was saying before about zoning out when I'm supposed to be grading or when I'm on the couch... I can sit in the exact same position for an hour or two without moving a muscle, just completely lost in whatever random thought process my brain has wandered off into. I definitely don't feel that physical urge to get up and pace around or burn off energy. Honestly, by the time the final bell rings at school, I don't feel like I have any excess energy to burn anyway. I usually just want to sink into a chair and not move for the rest of the evening. \n\nAnd as for the impulsivity part of your question... no, I wouldn't say that fits me either. I am definitely not the kind of person to just jump into things without thinking or make rash decisions on the fly. If anything, I probably overthink things, or I just don't take action at all because I get stuck in my own head trying to figure out the absolute best way to do something, and then I just end up doing nothing. \n\nLike, just as an example, when we were trying to pick a color to paint the guest room last summer, I spent three entire weeks looking at paint swatches and reading online reviews of different brands of eggshell finishes, just completely going down this rabbit hole of interior design blogs, and I never actually bought the paint. My wife finally just went to the hardware store and picked a color herself because I was so paralyzed by thinking about it too much. So, yeah, I am not blurting things out or acting without thinking. It is really just the focus and the spacing out that is the problem for me, not any kind of physical restlessness or impulsive behavior.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child—say, in elementary school or middle school—did your teachers or parents ever describe you as hyperactive, overly talkative, or someone who had trouble sitting still in class or at home? Or did they ever mention that you acted without thinking or interrupted people a lot?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not at all. It kind of goes back to what I was just saying about those old report cards my mom kept in that giant plastic tub in the attic. Because, you know, as a teacher now, I know exactly the kind of kid you're talking about. The ones who blurt out answers before you even finish the question, or the ones who are constantly up sharpening their pencil five times a period, or just physically cannot stop talking to the person next to them... but that was never me. Not even a little bit. \n\nIf anything, I think my teachers probably liked having me in class because I was so quiet. I was never a behavior problem. I never interrupted anyone because, well, frankly, I was usually too lost in my own little world to even know what the conversation was about in the first place, let alone interrupt it. I was the kid staring out the window at the clouds, or... or drawing these little elaborate mazes in the margins of my notebook while the teacher was talking. \n\nI actually remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, she told my parents at parent-teacher conferences that I was a sweet boy, but a million miles away. That was the exact phrase she used, and my mom used to repeat it all the time. So, no, nobody ever called me hyperactive or overly talkative. I was basically just a piece of furniture in the classroom, you know? Just quietly sitting there at my desk, completely zoned out, definitely not bothering anyone, but also not really absorbing anything either. So yeah, the physical restlessness and the blurting things out, that just wasn't me back then, just like it isn't me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been formally evaluated or diagnosed with ADHD—attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder—either as a child or as an adult?\n\nPatient: No, actually. Never. Not once. Which, I mean, looking back on it now, considering everything we just talked about with the report cards and the absolute disaster zone of my backpack, you would really think someone would have flagged it, right? But no, I never saw a doctor or a school psychologist or anyone like that when I was a kid. \n\nI think... well, honestly, I really think it just comes down to the fact that I wasn't causing any trouble for anyone but myself. Back then—I was in elementary school in the late nineties, early two-thousands—and I feel like the only kids who ever got sent to the counselor's office or got evaluated for anything were the ones who were literally bouncing off the walls. The ones throwing erasers or refusing to stay in their seats, like the kids I was just talking about. Since I was just quietly sitting there drawing my little mazes or staring at the ceiling, I just completely flew under the radar. \n\nMy parents, you know, they meant well, but they just thought I needed to... well, apply myself more. That was the big phrase in our house. \"He's smart, he just needs to apply himself.\" They figured I was maybe a little lazy, definitely disorganized, but they never once thought it was a clinical thing that needed an actual official evaluation. They just thought I'd eventually grow out of it if they made me sit at the kitchen table and clean my folders out enough times. \n\nAnd then as an adult... I mean, you just kind of get used to your own brain, don't you? You just assume everyone else is struggling just as hard to get through their paperwork or pay attention in long staff meetings, and you figure they're just somehow better at hiding it or pushing through it than you are. I never even considered getting formally checked out for anything until very recently. It wasn't until the grading just started piling up so badly and my wife sort of gently suggested that maybe this wasn't just normal teacher burnout, that I even thought about talking to somebody. So, yeah, this right here, talking to you, is actually the very first time I've ever sat down with a professional to look into this. It's all completely new territory for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your backpack was disorganized as a child and that you sometimes struggle to start tasks even when you intend to. Beyond just the difficulty focusing once you sit down, do you find it hard to organize your materials, plan out how to tackle a big project, or actually get yourself to begin a task in the first place—or is it really just the staying-focused part that's the main issue once you do start?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because I think it really is just the staying-focused part that is the main issue for me. It's actually not the organizing or the planning at all. I mean, the backpack thing, that was when I was ten years old, and obviously I was a mess back then. But now, as a teacher, I kind of have to have my act together when it comes to organizing materials, or I wouldn't survive the first week of September. \n\nI actually think I'm pretty good at planning things out. I have my whole Google Drive set up with these color-coded folders for every single unit I teach—like, blue for the Enlightenment, green for the Industrial Revolution, all of that. I can sit down at the beginning of a semester and map out exactly what we're going to do for the next four months. I know how to break down a big project. I make these very detailed rubrics for my students, I have my lesson plans perfectly lined up, and I keep all my physical papers in these specific accordion folders so I never lose a kid's assignment. So, no, organizing the materials or figuring out how to tackle a project isn't really a problem for me at all. \n\nAnd as for getting started... I don't think I struggle with that either, really. I don't have that feeling of being paralyzed to begin. Like, if I have a stack of fifty essays to grade, I can physically get myself to sit down at the dining room table. I have my whole routine: I make a cup of dark roast coffee, I clear off all the mail from the table, I get my specific red pens out—I only use the Pilot G2s because the other ones smear too much—and I pull the essays out of my bag. I can open the first one and start reading. I have the intention, and I initiate the task just fine. \n\nBut then... that is exactly where the wheels just completely fall off. It's the staying with it. Once I'm actually in the middle of the task, that's when my brain just sort of unplugs. I'll read the student's title, I'll read the first paragraph, and by the second paragraph, my eyes are still moving over the words, but I'm not absorbing a single thing. I'll suddenly realize I'm thinking about whether my car needs an oil change, or I'll just be staring blankly at the edge of the paper for twenty minutes without even realizing time is passing. \n\nSo it's not that I don't know how to plan the work, or that my desk is a disaster, or that I can't force myself to sit down and start. I have all the tools and the plans right there in front of me, ready to go. It is literally just keeping my brain locked onto the paper once I'm there. That is the part that feels completely impossible, no matter how well I've organized everything beforehand.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something you genuinely enjoy—like reading a book you're really into, or watching a show you're excited about, or even teaching a lesson you're passionate about in the classroom—do you find yourself having the same trouble staying focused, or is your attention actually pretty solid when you're interested in what you're doing?\n\nPatient: That’s... yeah, that’s a really interesting distinction to make, actually, because I’ve thought about that a lot. When I’m up in front of the classroom and we’re doing a lesson I’m incredibly passionate about—like, say, if we’re covering the French Revolution, which is absolutely my favorite unit to teach, I have this whole interactive simulation I do with the kids—I am completely locked in. I don’t lose focus at all. I’m engaged, I’m reacting to their questions, time just completely flies by. Or, you know, same thing if I’m watching a really incredible movie or reading a sci-fi novel that just totally grabs me from page one. I can sit there for hours and not even notice the sun going down outside. My attention is completely solid then.\n\nBut... see, the problem is that you can’t live your whole life just doing the highly entertaining, highly stimulating stuff, right? And honestly, that is exactly why I finally made the appointment to come in here and talk to you. Because the other parts of my life—the necessary parts, even the parts I genuinely want to do well—are just... they're destroying me. \n\nI came in because I can sit down at my dining room table with a stack of fifty sophomore history essays, or maybe a brand new district curriculum document that I really need to review for the upcoming semester. I have my coffee, I have my favorite pen, I am fully, one-hundred-percent intending to work. I want to get it done. But then... I swear, within ten minutes, it’s like a switch just flips in my head. I’ll suddenly snap back to reality and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page, maybe even the exact same sentence, for I don't even know how long. And my brain is just off in another universe, thinking about three completely unrelated things at once—like whether I remembered to take the chicken out of the freezer for dinner, or an email I need to send to my department head, or some random documentary about deep-sea fish I watched three years ago. \n\nI’m just sitting there, physically looking at the paper, but barely taking in a single word of what’s right in front of me. So even though I can focus on the fun stuff, the day-to-day reality of sitting down and sustaining that attention on my actual work is just... it's feeling completely impossible right now, and I just can't keep living like this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

This will help clarify the ADHD presentation and rule out other attention-related disorders.",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hmm... no. No, honestly, never. I really wish I could say yes, because if I could just somehow trick my brain into getting completely absorbed in a stack of sophomore essays on the causes of World War I—which, by the way, is a historical topic I actually love, but reading fifty teenagers write the exact same slightly-garbled paragraph about the assassination of Archduke Franz Ferdinand gets old incredibly fast—but if I could get lost in that, my life would be infinitely easier. 

But no, that kind of deep, lose-track-of-time focus? That is strictly, one-hundred-percent reserved for things I actually want to be doing. When it comes to the mundane stuff, or the required paperwork, it's the exact opposite of getting absorbed. It's like... it's like my brain is actively repelling the information. 

Take those district curriculum documents you just mentioned. Just last month, the administration sent out this massive, eighty-page PDF about the new standardized testing rubrics for the social studies department. Just dry as dust, the most bureaucratic language you can possibly imagine. I sat down on a Sunday afternoon, told myself I was going to power through it so I wouldn't have to worry about it during the week. I had the whole afternoon blocked off, I had my coffee, the house was quiet because my wife was out running errands. And I sat there for three hours, and I swear to you, I maybe read four pages. 

The whole time, I was just agonizing over every single sentence, reading the exact same paragraph about "competency-based metrics" over and over again until the words just started looking like random shapes on the screen. There was absolutely no flow, no losing track of time in a good way—just me staring at the clock on my laptop, feeling like every single minute was an hour, and getting absolutely nowhere. I ended up organizing my entire spice rack alphabetically just to escape it. 

So, yeah, to answer your question, if it's not a great sci-fi novel or a lesson I'm absolutely thrilled to teach, that deep focus just does not exist for me at all. It's like pulling teeth. I have never once in my entire career looked up from grading a stack of papers and thought, "Wow, where did the time go?" It's always more like, "How on earth has it only been twelve minutes?"

That is strictly, one-hundred-percent reserved for things I actually want to be doing. When it comes to the mundane stuff, or the required paperwork, it's the exact opposite of getting absorbed. It's like... it's like my brain is actively repelling the information. \n\nTake those district curriculum documents you just mentioned. Just last month, the administration sent out this massive, eighty-page PDF about the new standardized testing rubrics for the social studies department. Just dry as dust, the most bureaucratic language you can possibly imagine. I sat down on a Sunday afternoon, told myself I was going to power through it so I wouldn't have to worry about it during the week. I had the whole afternoon blocked off, I had my coffee, the house was quiet because my wife was out running errands. And I sat there for three hours, and I swear to you, I maybe read four pages. \n\nThe whole time, I was just agonizing over every single sentence, reading the exact same paragraph about \"competency-based metrics\" over and over again until the words just started looking like random shapes on the screen. There was absolutely no flow, no losing track of time in a good way—just me staring at the clock on my laptop, feeling like every single minute was an hour, and getting absolutely nowhere. I ended up organizing my entire spice rack alphabetically just to escape it. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, if it's not a great sci-fi novel or a lesson I'm absolutely thrilled to teach, that deep focus just does not exist for me at all. It's like pulling teeth. I have never once in my entire career looked up from grading a stack of papers and thought, \"Wow, where did the time go?\" It's always more like, \"How on earth has it only been twelve minutes?\"\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I forget ordinary responsibilities more than I want to admit, like replying to a parent email, paying a bill before the late notice comes, or picking up something my wife asked for on the way home.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in primary school, my report cards said things like “bright but careless” and “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised,” and my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled in my backpack that I had forgotten to turn in.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work, the missed follow-through has become serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan, and at home my wife has started taking over bills and reminders because she can’t count on me to keep track of them.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably worse for about 11 months, since the school changed the curriculum calendar and added the after-school planning meetings.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S002\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: You mentioned that your backpack was disorganized as a child and that you sometimes struggle to start tasks even when you intend to. Beyond just the difficulty focusing once you sit down, do you find it hard to organize your materials, plan out how to tackle a big project, or actually get yourself to begin a task in the first place—or is it really just the staying-focused part that's the main issue once you do start?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because I think it really is just the staying-focused part that is the main issue for me. It's actually not the organizing or the planning at all. I mean, the backpack thing, that was when I was ten years old, and obviously I was a mess back then. But now, as a teacher, I kind of have to have my act together when it comes to organizing materials, or I wouldn't survive the first week of September. \n\nI actually think I'm pretty good at planning things out. I have my whole Google Drive set up with these color-coded folders for every single unit I teach—like, blue for the Enlightenment, green for the Industrial Revolution, all of that. I can sit down at the beginning of a semester and map out exactly what we're going to do for the next four months. I know how to break down a big project. I make these very detailed rubrics for my students, I have my lesson plans perfectly lined up, and I keep all my physical papers in these specific accordion folders so I never lose a kid's assignment. So, no, organizing the materials or figuring out how to tackle a project isn't really a problem for me at all. \n\nAnd as for getting started... I don't think I struggle with that either, really. I don't have that feeling of being paralyzed to begin. Like, if I have a stack of fifty essays to grade, I can physically get myself to sit down at the dining room table. I have my whole routine: I make a cup of dark roast coffee, I clear off all the mail from the table, I get my specific red pens out—I only use the Pilot G2s because the other ones smear too much—and I pull the essays out of my bag. I can open the first one and start reading. I have the intention, and I initiate the task just fine. \n\nBut then... that is exactly where the wheels just completely fall off. It's the staying with it. Once I'm actually in the middle of the task, that's when my brain just sort of unplugs. I'll read the student's title, I'll read the first paragraph, and by the second paragraph, my eyes are still moving over the words, but I'm not absorbing a single thing. I'll suddenly realize I'm thinking about whether my car needs an oil change, or I'll just be staring blankly at the edge of the paper for twenty minutes without even realizing time is passing. \n\nSo it's not that I don't know how to plan the work, or that my desk is a disaster, or that I can't force myself to sit down and start. I have all the tools and the plans right there in front of me, ready to go. It is literally just keeping my brain locked onto the paper once I'm there. That is the part that feels completely impossible, no matter how well I've organized everything beforehand.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something you genuinely enjoy—like reading a book you're really into, or watching a show you're excited about, or even teaching a lesson you're passionate about in the classroom—do you find yourself having the same trouble staying focused, or is your attention actually pretty solid when you're interested in what you're doing?\n\nPatient: That’s... yeah, that’s a really interesting distinction to make, actually, because I’ve thought about that a lot. When I’m up in front of the classroom and we’re doing a lesson I’m incredibly passionate about—like, say, if we’re covering the French Revolution, which is absolutely my favorite unit to teach, I have this whole interactive simulation I do with the kids—I am completely locked in. I don’t lose focus at all. I’m engaged, I’m reacting to their questions, time just completely flies by. Or, you know, same thing if I’m watching a really incredible movie or reading a sci-fi novel that just totally grabs me from page one. I can sit there for hours and not even notice the sun going down outside. My attention is completely solid then.\n\nBut... see, the problem is that you can’t live your whole life just doing the highly entertaining, highly stimulating stuff, right? And honestly, that is exactly why I finally made the appointment to come in here and talk to you. Because the other parts of my life—the necessary parts, even the parts I genuinely want to do well—are just... they're destroying me. \n\nI came in because I can sit down at my dining room table with a stack of fifty sophomore history essays, or maybe a brand new district curriculum document that I really need to review for the upcoming semester. I have my coffee, I have my favorite pen, I am fully, one-hundred-percent intending to work. I want to get it done. But then... I swear, within ten minutes, it’s like a switch just flips in my head. I’ll suddenly snap back to reality and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page, maybe even the exact same sentence, for I don't even know how long. And my brain is just off in another universe, thinking about three completely unrelated things at once—like whether I remembered to take the chicken out of the freezer for dinner, or an email I need to send to my department head, or some random documentary about deep-sea fish I watched three years ago. \n\nI’m just sitting there, physically looking at the paper, but barely taking in a single word of what’s right in front of me. So even though I can focus on the fun stuff, the day-to-day reality of sitting down and sustaining that attention on my actual work is just... it's feeling completely impossible right now, and I just can't keep living like this.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that you can focus really well on things you're passionate about, like teaching your favorite unit or reading a book you love. But I'm curious about something else: have you ever experienced a time when you were doing something that wasn't particularly fun or stimulating—like grading papers or reading a required document—where you actually got completely absorbed in it and lost track of time? Or does that kind of deep focus only happen with things you're genuinely interested in?\n\nPatient: Hmm... no. No, honestly, never. I really wish I could say yes, because if I could just somehow trick my brain into getting completely absorbed in a stack of sophomore essays on the causes of World War I—which, by the way, is a historical topic I actually love, but reading fifty teenagers write the exact same slightly-garbled paragraph about the assassination of Archduke Franz Ferdinand gets old incredibly fast—but if I could get lost in that, my life would be infinitely easier. \n\nBut no, that kind of deep, lose-track-of-time focus? That is strictly, one-hundred-percent reserved for things I actually want to be doing. When it comes to the mundane stuff, or the required paperwork, it's the exact opposite of getting absorbed. It's like... it's like my brain is actively repelling the information. \n\nTake those district curriculum documents you just mentioned. Just last month, the administration sent out this massive, eighty-page PDF about the new standardized testing rubrics for the social studies department. Just dry as dust, the most bureaucratic language you can possibly imagine. I sat down on a Sunday afternoon, told myself I was going to power through it so I wouldn't have to worry about it during the week. I had the whole afternoon blocked off, I had my coffee, the house was quiet because my wife was out running errands. And I sat there for three hours, and I swear to you, I maybe read four pages. \n\nThe whole time, I was just agonizing over every single sentence, reading the exact same paragraph about \"competency-based metrics\" over and over again until the words just started looking like random shapes on the screen. There was absolutely no flow, no losing track of time in a good way—just me staring at the clock on my laptop, feeling like every single minute was an hour, and getting absolutely nowhere. I ended up organizing my entire spice rack alphabetically just to escape it. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, if it's not a great sci-fi novel or a lesson I'm absolutely thrilled to teach, that deep focus just does not exist for me at all. It's like pulling teeth. I have never once in my entire career looked up from grading a stack of papers and thought, \"Wow, where did the time go?\" It's always more like, \"How on earth has it only been twelve minutes?\"\n\n[Last Question]\nGiven everything you've described—the lifelong attention struggles, the difficulty sustaining focus on non-preferred tasks, and how much this is affecting your work and your marriage—have you ever actually been referred for a formal ADHD evaluation, or has that just never come up in any conversations with doctors?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, honestly, it’s never... I’ve never been referred for anything like that. It just never came up in any doctor's office, ever. Which, looking back, is kind of wild, but I guess when I was a kid in the nineties, you know, the kids who got evaluated for that sort of thing were the ones bouncing off the walls, throwing chairs, or disrupting the whole class. I was never like that. I was the quiet kid in the back row staring out the window at the clouds, so my teachers just wrote on my report cards that I had so much potential if I would just apply myself, or that I needed to focus more. My parents just thought I was disorganized and maybe a little lazy, to be perfectly honest. So a doctor was never involved back then. 

And then as an adult... well, you know how it is, you go to your primary care guy for a sinus infection or to get a physical for life insurance, and you get maybe seven minutes with them. They check your blood pressure, ask if you smoke, and that’s it. I am not sitting there in the crinkly paper gown thinking about telling him that I spent three hours alphabetically organizing my spice rack instead of reading a district PDF, or asking if there is something wrong with my brain. It just doesn't feel like the right venue for that kind of deep conversation, especially when there are three other people coughing in the waiting room. So I never brought it up, and they obviously never asked. 

It really wasn't until my wife... I mean, Sarah has been incredibly patient with me, she really has, but a couple months ago we had this massive argument because I completely forgot to pay the property taxes. I had the bill right on my desk, literally right next to my coffee mug, but I got distracted by grading those exact same sophomore essays we were just talking about, and the bill just vanished from my reality. She was so frustrated, and she finally just told me flat out that I needed to talk to a professional about my attention, because living like this isn't normal and she can't keep being my manager on top of her own job. That was really the wake-up call for me to seek out someone like you. But before making this specific appointment? No, no medical professional has ever mentioned an evaluation to me. Not once.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well, honestly, it's... it's been a long time coming, I think. I guess the main reason I finally made the appointment is because of how things are going with my job. I'm a secondary school teacher, which I actually really enjoy—the kids are great, mostly, and being in the classroom is fine—but the behind-the-scenes stuff is just becoming this impossible mountain for me. \n\nLike, I will literally sit down at my dining room table with a massive stack of essays. I even have this whole routine where I make a cup of coffee, clear away all the clutter so I won't get distracted, get my grading pens out, and I sit down fully, one hundred percent intending to work. I’ll tell myself, 'Okay, you are going to grade just five of these before you get up.' But then... I don't know, maybe ten minutes will go by, and I’ll suddenly sort of snap to and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page the entire time. \n\nI’ll be looking right at the words, but I'm barely taking in what’s in front of me. Instead, I'm sitting there thinking about three completely unrelated things—like whether I remembered to reply to a parent's email from Tuesday, or what I need to pick up from the grocery store later, or some random documentary I watched over the weekend. And it happens with curriculum documents, too. I try to read through the state standards for the next unit, and my eyes just glaze over. It’s incredibly frustrating because I have the absolute best intentions to just get the work done, but I end up trapped there, staring at the paper, and an hour will pass with zero progress. It's just exhausting having to fight my own brain just to read a single essay.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting there unable to focus on your work, do you notice that you're feeling worried or anxious about anything—either about the work itself, or about other things in general?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not... I wouldn't say it's anxiety. Like, I know exactly what you mean, because my sister actually gets really anxious about her job and she talks about this intense sense of dread she gets, like her chest gets tight and she can't breathe. But for me, it's not like that at all. I'm not sitting there worrying about failing or stressing over the quality of the students' work. \n\nIt's more like... my brain just quietly slips out the back door while I'm not looking. I’m not sitting there agonizing over the essays, or stressing about parents being mad at me, or even worrying about general life stuff like money or whatever. I mean, sure, I'll think about the grocery store, like I mentioned before, but it's not a panicked, 'Oh no, I'm so worried I'll forget the milk!' kind of thought. It's just this totally neutral, completely unhelpful tangent. I'll just be sitting there thinking, 'Hmm, I wonder if they still have that specific brand of oat milk on sale, because last time they were out, and then I had to go to the other store down on 4th street, which is always an absolute nightmare for parking, especially on a Tuesday...' \n\nSee? It's just this endless train of thought that goes on and on, completely peacefully, while the essay just sits there ignored. So, yeah, no racing heart or sweaty palms or that nervous pit in your stomach. Honestly, sometimes I'm almost too calm about it in the moment. I'm just totally zoned out, thinking about, I don't know, the history of the Roman Empire or how they manufacture glass, or whatever random rabbit hole my brain decided was infinitely more interesting than a 9th grader's thesis statement on To Kill a Mockingbird. \n\nIt’s really only *afterward*, when I snap out of it and look at the clock and realize an entire hour has passed and I haven't capped my red pen once, that I get frustrated with myself. But in the actual moment when the distraction is happening? No, no worry or anxiety at all. Just... totally adrift.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these kinds of concentration problems in other areas of your life—like when you're reading for pleasure, watching a movie, having a conversation with a friend, or doing hobbies you actually enjoy—or is it really just happening when you're trying to do work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is... I wish it was just the grading, honestly. Because if it was just the paperwork, maybe I could just find a better system or force myself through it somehow. But no, it definitely bleeds into other areas, especially when I am supposed to be listening to people. It is actually becoming a pretty big issue, to be honest. \n\nLike, for example, we have these weekly planning meetings for the English department. They are usually on Wednesday afternoons right after the kids leave, so everyone is already a bit drained, but they are important because we have to align our lesson plans for the following week. And my department head will be going over something, like the new grading rubric for the district writing assessments. And I am sitting there, I am looking right at her, making solid eye contact, and I am even nodding at all the appropriate times. I look like the most engaged teacher in the room. But internally? I am completely gone. And the worst part, the absolute worst part, is when she suddenly stops and asks me what I think about adjusting the weighting on the grammar section, or something like that, and I just freeze. Because I realize I have absolutely no idea what she just said. I have completely missed the actual point of the last five minutes of her talking. \n\nAnd it happens at home, too, which is even harder to deal with. I will be sitting on the couch with my wife after dinner, just trying to unwind, and she will be telling me about her day or trying to figure out our weekend plans. And it is the exact same thing. I will be looking right at her, nodding along, giving her all the right physical cues that I am listening. But my brain has just... detached. It is like the audio is playing in the room, but my brain is not recording any of it. Then she will ask me which day I think works better for us to go to her parents house, and I just have to sit there and admit that I missed the entire point of what she was just saying. \n\nShe gets so frustrated, and I really do not blame her at all, because from the outside it looks like I just do not care enough to listen. But I do care. I really want to hear about her day, and I want to be a good partner, but it is like my brain just refuses to stay anchored to the conversation. I will just drift off into thinking about the yard work or a podcast I listened to on the drive home, all while staring right at her. So, yeah, it is definitely not just the work tasks. It is everyday conversations, too, which really makes me feel terrible.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these concentration and attention problems? Did they start suddenly at some point, or have they been something you've dealt with for most of your life?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because for a long time I really thought this was just a recent thing. I kept telling myself that I was just burned out from teaching, or that the curriculum changes were just getting to me, but... well, when I actually sat down and really thought about it, and especially after talking to my mom a while back, I realized this has basically been my normal for my entire life. It definitely wasn't sudden at all. \n\nI mean, going all the way back to primary school, it was happening. My mom is one of those people who keeps absolutely everything, so she has this massive plastic tub in their attic with all my childhood stuff, and we were actually looking through my old report cards not that long ago. And it was honestly kind of wild to read them now, with the perspective I have as a teacher myself, because every single year, from like first grade all the way up, my teachers were writing the exact same comments. Over and over, the remarks were always things like, “bright but careless,” or they’d write that I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” Which, you know, is exactly what I was just saying about needing to force myself to sit at the dining room table to grade, right? \n\nAnd it wasn't just the report cards, either. I distinctly remember the absolute state of my backpack back then. It was a complete disaster zone. My parents would make me sit at the kitchen table on Friday afternoons and they would just dump the whole bag out upside down to clean it, and they were always, without fail, finding all these worksheets just shoved at the bottom, completely crumpled up. And the most frustrating part—for them and for me—was that a lot of the time, I had actually done the work! I had sat there and done the math problems or the spelling words, but I just completely forgot to actually take the paper out of my bag and put it in the tray to turn it in. \n\nMy dad would get so exasperated, just sitting there trying to smooth out these incredibly wrinkled, destroyed pieces of paper so I could hand them in late. So my grades would suffer not because I didn't understand the lessons, but because there was this total disconnect between doing the task and actually finishing the final step. So, yeah, to answer your question, it’s definitely not a new development. It has always been there. I think the only difference now is that I don't have my parents or a teacher standing over my shoulder making sure I empty my backpack or finish my work. I'm the adult now, so all that structure is gone, and I'm just left dealing with the exact same brain I had when I was seven.\n\nDoctor: Growing up and even now, have you noticed that you tend to be restless or fidgety—like needing to move around a lot, or feeling like you have excess energy that's hard to contain? Or do you find yourself acting on impulses without thinking things through first?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I see that kind of thing all the time with my students, obviously. You get a classroom full of fourteen-year-olds and it is just a constant, never-ending symphony of tapping pencils and bouncing knees and kids asking to go to the bathroom just so they have an excuse to walk down the hall. I have this one kid in my third period, actually, who literally cannot keep all four legs of his chair on the floor for more than three minutes at a time. It is like he is physically vibrating with all this pent-up energy, just constantly shifting and moving. \n\nBut for me? No, I don't get that feeling at all. I am actually the complete opposite. If anything, I can sit entirely too still. Like I was saying before about zoning out when I'm supposed to be grading or when I'm on the couch... I can sit in the exact same position for an hour or two without moving a muscle, just completely lost in whatever random thought process my brain has wandered off into. I definitely don't feel that physical urge to get up and pace around or burn off energy. Honestly, by the time the final bell rings at school, I don't feel like I have any excess energy to burn anyway. I usually just want to sink into a chair and not move for the rest of the evening. \n\nAnd as for the impulsivity part of your question... no, I wouldn't say that fits me either. I am definitely not the kind of person to just jump into things without thinking or make rash decisions on the fly. If anything, I probably overthink things, or I just don't take action at all because I get stuck in my own head trying to figure out the absolute best way to do something, and then I just end up doing nothing. \n\nLike, just as an example, when we were trying to pick a color to paint the guest room last summer, I spent three entire weeks looking at paint swatches and reading online reviews of different brands of eggshell finishes, just completely going down this rabbit hole of interior design blogs, and I never actually bought the paint. My wife finally just went to the hardware store and picked a color herself because I was so paralyzed by thinking about it too much. So, yeah, I am not blurting things out or acting without thinking. It is really just the focus and the spacing out that is the problem for me, not any kind of physical restlessness or impulsive behavior.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child—say, in elementary school or middle school—did your teachers or parents ever describe you as hyperactive, overly talkative, or someone who had trouble sitting still in class or at home? Or did they ever mention that you acted without thinking or interrupted people a lot?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not at all. It kind of goes back to what I was just saying about those old report cards my mom kept in that giant plastic tub in the attic. Because, you know, as a teacher now, I know exactly the kind of kid you're talking about. The ones who blurt out answers before you even finish the question, or the ones who are constantly up sharpening their pencil five times a period, or just physically cannot stop talking to the person next to them... but that was never me. Not even a little bit. \n\nIf anything, I think my teachers probably liked having me in class because I was so quiet. I was never a behavior problem. I never interrupted anyone because, well, frankly, I was usually too lost in my own little world to even know what the conversation was about in the first place, let alone interrupt it. I was the kid staring out the window at the clouds, or... or drawing these little elaborate mazes in the margins of my notebook while the teacher was talking. \n\nI actually remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, she told my parents at parent-teacher conferences that I was a sweet boy, but a million miles away. That was the exact phrase she used, and my mom used to repeat it all the time. So, no, nobody ever called me hyperactive or overly talkative. I was basically just a piece of furniture in the classroom, you know? Just quietly sitting there at my desk, completely zoned out, definitely not bothering anyone, but also not really absorbing anything either. So yeah, the physical restlessness and the blurting things out, that just wasn't me back then, just like it isn't me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been formally evaluated or diagnosed with ADHD—attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder—either as a child or as an adult?\n\nPatient: No, actually. Never. Not once. Which, I mean, looking back on it now, considering everything we just talked about with the report cards and the absolute disaster zone of my backpack, you would really think someone would have flagged it, right? But no, I never saw a doctor or a school psychologist or anyone like that when I was a kid. \n\nI think... well, honestly, I really think it just comes down to the fact that I wasn't causing any trouble for anyone but myself. Back then—I was in elementary school in the late nineties, early two-thousands—and I feel like the only kids who ever got sent to the counselor's office or got evaluated for anything were the ones who were literally bouncing off the walls. The ones throwing erasers or refusing to stay in their seats, like the kids I was just talking about. Since I was just quietly sitting there drawing my little mazes or staring at the ceiling, I just completely flew under the radar. \n\nMy parents, you know, they meant well, but they just thought I needed to... well, apply myself more. That was the big phrase in our house. \"He's smart, he just needs to apply himself.\" They figured I was maybe a little lazy, definitely disorganized, but they never once thought it was a clinical thing that needed an actual official evaluation. They just thought I'd eventually grow out of it if they made me sit at the kitchen table and clean my folders out enough times. \n\nAnd then as an adult... I mean, you just kind of get used to your own brain, don't you? You just assume everyone else is struggling just as hard to get through their paperwork or pay attention in long staff meetings, and you figure they're just somehow better at hiding it or pushing through it than you are. I never even considered getting formally checked out for anything until very recently. It wasn't until the grading just started piling up so badly and my wife sort of gently suggested that maybe this wasn't just normal teacher burnout, that I even thought about talking to somebody. So, yeah, this right here, talking to you, is actually the very first time I've ever sat down with a professional to look into this. It's all completely new territory for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your backpack was disorganized as a child and that you sometimes struggle to start tasks even when you intend to. Beyond just the difficulty focusing once you sit down, do you find it hard to organize your materials, plan out how to tackle a big project, or actually get yourself to begin a task in the first place—or is it really just the staying-focused part that's the main issue once you do start?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because I think it really is just the staying-focused part that is the main issue for me. It's actually not the organizing or the planning at all. I mean, the backpack thing, that was when I was ten years old, and obviously I was a mess back then. But now, as a teacher, I kind of have to have my act together when it comes to organizing materials, or I wouldn't survive the first week of September. \n\nI actually think I'm pretty good at planning things out. I have my whole Google Drive set up with these color-coded folders for every single unit I teach—like, blue for the Enlightenment, green for the Industrial Revolution, all of that. I can sit down at the beginning of a semester and map out exactly what we're going to do for the next four months. I know how to break down a big project. I make these very detailed rubrics for my students, I have my lesson plans perfectly lined up, and I keep all my physical papers in these specific accordion folders so I never lose a kid's assignment. So, no, organizing the materials or figuring out how to tackle a project isn't really a problem for me at all. \n\nAnd as for getting started... I don't think I struggle with that either, really. I don't have that feeling of being paralyzed to begin. Like, if I have a stack of fifty essays to grade, I can physically get myself to sit down at the dining room table. It's the staying with it. Once I'm actually in the middle of the task, that's when my brain just sort of unplugs. I'll read the student's title, I'll read the first paragraph, and by the second paragraph, my eyes are still moving over the words, but I'm not absorbing a single thing. I'll suddenly realize I'm thinking about whether my car needs an oil change, or I'll just be staring blankly at the edge of the paper for twenty minutes without even realizing time is passing. \n\nSo it's not that I don't know how to plan the work, or that my desk is a disaster, or that I can't force myself to sit down and start. That is the part that feels completely impossible, no matter how well I've organized everything beforehand.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something you genuinely enjoy—like reading a book you're really into, or watching a show you're excited about, or even teaching a lesson you're passionate about in the classroom—do you find yourself having the same trouble staying focused, or is your attention actually pretty solid when you're interested in what you're doing?\n\nPatient: That’s... yeah, that’s a really interesting distinction to make, actually, because I’ve thought about that a lot. I can sit there for hours and not even notice the sun going down outside. I really wish I could say yes, because if I could just somehow trick my brain into getting completely absorbed in a stack of sophomore essays on the causes of World War I—which, by the way, is a historical topic I actually love, but reading fifty teenagers write the exact same slightly-garbled paragraph about the assassination of Archduke Franz Ferdinand gets old incredibly fast—but if I could get lost in that, my life would be infinitely easier. \n\nBut no, that kind of deep, lose-track-of-time focus? Or do you find yourself acting on impulses without thinking things through first?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I see that kind of thing all the time with my students, obviously. You get a classroom full of fourteen-year-olds and it is just a constant, never-ending symphony of tapping pencils and bouncing knees and kids asking to go to the bathroom just so they have an excuse to walk down the hall. I have this one kid in my third period, actually, who literally cannot keep all four legs of his chair on the floor for more than three minutes at a time. It is like he is physically vibrating with all this pent-up energy, just constantly shifting and moving. \n\nBut for me? No, I don't get that feeling at all. I am actually the complete opposite. If anything, I can sit entirely too still. Like I was saying before about zoning out when I'm supposed to be grading or when I'm on the couch... I can sit in the exact same position for an hour or two without moving a muscle, just completely lost in whatever random thought process my brain has wandered off into. I definitely don't feel that physical urge to get up and pace around or burn off energy. Honestly, by the time the final bell rings at school, I don't feel like I have any excess energy to burn anyway. I usually just want to sink into a chair and not move for the rest of the evening. \n\nAnd as for the impulsivity part of your question... no, I wouldn't say that fits me either. I am definitely not the kind of person to just jump into things without thinking or make rash decisions on the fly. If anything, I probably overthink things, or I just don't take action at all because I get stuck in my own head trying to figure out the absolute best way to do something, and then I just end up doing nothing. \n\nLike, just as an example, when we were trying to pick a color to paint the guest room last summer, I spent three entire weeks looking at paint swatches and reading online reviews of different brands of eggshell finishes, just completely going down this rabbit hole of interior design blogs, and I never actually bought the paint. My wife finally just went to the hardware store and picked a color herself because I was so paralyzed by thinking about it too much. So, yeah, I am not blurting things out or acting without thinking. It is really just the focus and the spacing out that is the problem for me, not any kind of physical restlessness or impulsive behavior.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child—say, in elementary school or middle school—did your teachers or parents ever describe you as hyperactive, overly talkative, or someone who had trouble sitting still in class or at home? Or did they ever mention that you acted without thinking or interrupted people a lot?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, not at all. It kind of goes back to what I was just saying about those old report cards my mom kept in that giant plastic tub in the attic. Because, you know, as a teacher now, I know exactly the kind of kid you're talking about. The ones who blurt out answers before you even finish the question, or the ones who are constantly up sharpening their pencil five times a period, or just physically cannot stop talking to the person next to them... but that was never me. Not even a little bit. \n\nIf anything, I think my teachers probably liked having me in class because I was so quiet. I was never a behavior problem. I never interrupted anyone because, well, frankly, I was usually too lost in my own little world to even know what the conversation was about in the first place, let alone interrupt it. I was the kid staring out the window at the clouds, or... or drawing these little elaborate mazes in the margins of my notebook while the teacher was talking. \n\nI actually remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, she told my parents at parent-teacher conferences that I was a sweet boy, but a million miles away. That was the exact phrase she used, and my mom used to repeat it all the time. So, no, nobody ever called me hyperactive or overly talkative. I was basically just a piece of furniture in the classroom, you know? Just quietly sitting there at my desk, completely zoned out, definitely not bothering anyone, but also not really absorbing anything either. So yeah, the physical restlessness and the blurting things out, that just wasn't me back then, just like it isn't me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been formally evaluated or diagnosed with ADHD—attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder—either as a child or as an adult?\n\nPatient: No, actually. Never. Not once. Which, I mean, looking back on it now, considering everything we just talked about with the report cards and the absolute disaster zone of my backpack, you would really think someone would have flagged it, right? But no, I never saw a doctor or a school psychologist or anyone like that when I was a kid. \n\nI think... well, honestly, I really think it just comes down to the fact that I wasn't causing any trouble for anyone but myself. Back then—I was in elementary school in the late nineties, early two-thousands—and I feel like the only kids who ever got sent to the counselor's office or got evaluated for anything were the ones who were literally bouncing off the walls. The ones throwing erasers or refusing to stay in their seats, like the kids I was just talking about. Since I was just quietly sitting there drawing my little mazes or staring at the ceiling, I just completely flew under the radar. \n\nMy parents, you know, they meant well, but they just thought I needed to... well, apply myself more. That was the big phrase in our house. \"He's smart, he just needs to apply himself.\" They figured I was maybe a little lazy, definitely disorganized, but they never once thought it was a clinical thing that needed an actual official evaluation. They just thought I'd eventually grow out of it if they made me sit at the kitchen table and clean my folders out enough times. \n\nAnd then as an adult... I mean, you just kind of get used to your own brain, don't you? You just assume everyone else is struggling just as hard to get through their paperwork or pay attention in long staff meetings, and you figure they're just somehow better at hiding it or pushing through it than you are. I never even considered getting formally checked out for anything until very recently. It wasn't until the grading just started piling up so badly and my wife sort of gently suggested that maybe this wasn't just normal teacher burnout, that I even thought about talking to somebody. So, yeah, this right here, talking to you, is actually the very first time I've ever sat down with a professional to look into this. It's all completely new territory for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your backpack was disorganized as a child and that you sometimes struggle to start tasks even when you intend to. Beyond just the difficulty focusing once you sit down, do you find it hard to organize your materials, plan out how to tackle a big project, or actually get yourself to begin a task in the first place—or is it really just the staying-focused part that's the main issue once you do start?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because I think it really is just the staying-focused part that is the main issue for me. It's actually not the organizing or the planning at all. I mean, the backpack thing, that was when I was ten years old, and obviously I was a mess back then. But now, as a teacher, I kind of have to have my act together when it comes to organizing materials, or I wouldn't survive the first week of September. \n\nI actually think I'm pretty good at planning things out. I have my whole Google Drive set up with these color-coded folders for every single unit I teach—like, blue for the Enlightenment, green for the Industrial Revolution, all of that. I can sit down at the beginning of a semester and map out exactly what we're going to do for the next four months. I know how to break down a big project. I make these very detailed rubrics for my students, I have my lesson plans perfectly lined up, and I keep all my physical papers in these specific accordion folders so I never lose a kid's assignment. So, no, organizing the materials or figuring out how to tackle a project isn't really a problem for me at all. \n\nAnd as for getting started... I don't think I struggle with that either, really. I don't have that feeling of being paralyzed to begin. Like, if I have a stack of fifty essays to grade, I can physically get myself to sit down at the dining room table. I have my whole routine: I make a cup of dark roast coffee, I clear off all the mail from the table, I get my specific red pens out—I only use the Pilot G2s because the other ones smear too much—and I pull the essays out of my bag. I can open the first one and start reading. I have the intention, and I initiate the task just fine. \n\nBut then... that is exactly where the wheels just completely fall off. It's the staying with it. Once I'm actually in the middle of the task, that's when my brain just sort of unplugs. I'll read the student's title, I'll read the first paragraph, and by the second paragraph, my eyes are still moving over the words, but I'm not absorbing a single thing. I'll suddenly realize I'm thinking about whether my car needs an oil change, or I'll just be staring blankly at the edge of the paper for twenty minutes without even realizing time is passing. \n\nSo it's not that I don't know how to plan the work, or that my desk is a disaster, or that I can't force myself to sit down and start. I have all the tools and the plans right there in front of me, ready to go. It is literally just keeping my brain locked onto the paper once I'm there. That is the part that feels completely impossible, no matter how well I've organized everything beforehand.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something you genuinely enjoy—like reading a book you're really into, or watching a show you're excited about, or even teaching a lesson you're passionate about in the classroom—do you find yourself having the same trouble staying focused, or is your attention actually pretty solid when you're interested in what you're doing?\n\nPatient: That’s... yeah, that’s a really interesting distinction to make, actually, because I’ve thought about that a lot. When I’m up in front of the classroom and we’re doing a lesson I’m incredibly passionate about—like, say, if we’re covering the French Revolution, which is absolutely my favorite unit to teach, I have this whole interactive simulation I do with the kids—I am completely locked in. I don’t lose focus at all. I’m engaged, I’m reacting to their questions, time just completely flies by. Or, you know, same thing if I’m watching a really incredible movie or reading a sci-fi novel that just totally grabs me from page one. I can sit there for hours and not even notice the sun going down outside. My attention is completely solid then.\n\nBut... see, the problem is that you can’t live your whole life just doing the highly entertaining, highly stimulating stuff, right? And honestly, that is exactly why I finally made the appointment to come in here and talk to you. Because the other parts of my life—the necessary parts, even the parts I genuinely want to do well—are just... they're destroying me. \n\nI came in because I can sit down at my dining room table with a stack of fifty sophomore history essays, or maybe a brand new district curriculum document that I really need to review for the upcoming semester. I have my coffee, I have my favorite pen, I am fully, one-hundred-percent intending to work. I want to get it done. But then... I swear, within ten minutes, it’s like a switch just flips in my head. I’ll suddenly snap back to reality and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page, maybe even the exact same sentence, for I don't even know how long. And my brain is just off in another universe, thinking about three completely unrelated things at once—like whether I remembered to take the chicken out of the freezer for dinner, or an email I need to send to my department head, or some random documentary about deep-sea fish I watched three years ago. \n\nI’m just sitting there, physically looking at the paper, but barely taking in a single word of what’s right in front of me. So even though I can focus on the fun stuff, the day-to-day reality of sitting down and sustaining that attention on my actual work is just... it's feeling completely impossible right now, and I just can't keep living like this.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that you can focus really well on things you're passionate about, like teaching your favorite unit or reading a book you love. But I'm curious about something else: have you ever experienced a time when you were doing something that wasn't particularly fun or stimulating—like grading papers or reading a required document—where you actually got completely absorbed in it and lost track of time? Or does that kind of deep focus only happen with things you're genuinely interested in?\n\nPatient: Hmm... no. No, honestly, never. I really wish I could say yes, because if I could just somehow trick my brain into getting completely absorbed in a stack of sophomore essays on the causes of World War I—which, by the way, is a historical topic I actually love, but reading fifty teenagers write the exact same slightly-garbled paragraph about the assassination of Archduke Franz Ferdinand gets old incredibly fast—but if I could get lost in that, my life would be infinitely easier. \n\nBut no, that kind of deep, lose-track-of-time focus? That is strictly, one-hundred-percent reserved for things I actually want to be doing. When it comes to the mundane stuff, or the required paperwork, it's the exact opposite of getting absorbed. It's like... it's like my brain is actively repelling the information. \n\nTake those district curriculum documents you just mentioned. Just last month, the administration sent out this massive, eighty-page PDF about the new standardized testing rubrics for the social studies department. Just dry as dust, the most bureaucratic language you can possibly imagine. I sat down on a Sunday afternoon, told myself I was going to power through it so I wouldn't have to worry about it during the week. I had the whole afternoon blocked off, I had my coffee, the house was quiet because my wife was out running errands. And I sat there for three hours, and I swear to you, I maybe read four pages. \n\nThe whole time, I was just agonizing over every single sentence, reading the exact same paragraph about \"competency-based metrics\" over and over again until the words just started looking like random shapes on the screen. There was absolutely no flow, no losing track of time in a good way—just me staring at the clock on my laptop, feeling like every single minute was an hour, and getting absolutely nowhere. I ended up organizing my entire spice rack alphabetically just to escape it. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, if it's not a great sci-fi novel or a lesson I'm absolutely thrilled to teach, that deep focus just does not exist for me at all. It's like pulling teeth. I have never once in my entire career looked up from grading a stack of papers and thought, \"Wow, where did the time go?\" It's always more like, \"How on earth has it only been twelve minutes?\"\n\nDoctor: Given everything you've described—the lifelong attention struggles, the difficulty sustaining focus on non-preferred tasks, and how much this is affecting your work and your marriage—have you ever actually been referred for a formal ADHD evaluation, or has that just never come up in any conversations with doctors?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, it’s never... I’ve never been referred for anything like that. It just never came up in any doctor's office, ever. Which, looking back, is kind of wild, but I guess when I was a kid in the nineties, you know, the kids who got evaluated for that sort of thing were the ones bouncing off the walls, throwing chairs, or disrupting the whole class. I was never like that. I was the quiet kid in the back row staring out the window at the clouds, so my teachers just wrote on my report cards that I had so much potential if I would just apply myself, or that I needed to focus more. My parents just thought I was disorganized and maybe a little lazy, to be perfectly honest. So a doctor was never involved back then. \n\nAnd then as an adult... well, you know how it is, you go to your primary care guy for a sinus infection or to get a physical for life insurance, and you get maybe seven minutes with them. They check your blood pressure, ask if you smoke, and that’s it. I am not sitting there in the crinkly paper gown thinking about telling him that I spent three hours alphabetically organizing my spice rack instead of reading a district PDF, or asking if there is something wrong with my brain. It just doesn't feel like the right venue for that kind of deep conversation, especially when there are three other people coughing in the waiting room. So I never brought it up, and they obviously never asked. \n\nIt really wasn't until my wife... I mean, Sarah has been incredibly patient with me, she really has, but a couple months ago we had this massive argument because I completely forgot to pay the property taxes. I had the bill right on my desk, literally right next to my coffee mug, but I got distracted by grading those exact same sophomore essays we were just talking about, and the bill just vanished from my reality. She was so frustrated, and she finally just told me flat out that I needed to talk to a professional about my attention, because living like this isn't normal and she can't keep being my manager on top of her own job. That was really the wake-up call for me to seek out someone like you. But before making this specific appointment? No, no medical professional has ever mentioned an evaluation to me. Not once.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
