I’ll suddenly snap back to reality and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page, maybe even the exact same sentence, for I don't even know how long. And my brain is just off in another universe, thinking about three completely unrelated things at once—like whether I remembered to take the chicken out of the freezer for dinner, or an email I need to send to my department head, or some random documentary about deep-sea fish I watched three years ago. \n\nI’m just sitting there, physically looking at the paper, but barely taking in a single word of what’s right in front of me. So even though I can focus on the fun stuff, the day-to-day reality of sitting down and sustaining that attention on my actual work is just... it's feeling completely impossible right now, and I just can't keep living like this.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that you can focus really well on things you're passionate about, like teaching your favorite unit or reading a book you love. But I'm curious about something else: have you ever experienced a time when you were doing something that wasn't particularly fun or stimulating—like grading papers or reading a required document—where you actually got completely absorbed in it and lost track of time? Or does that kind of deep focus only happen with things you're genuinely interested in?\n\nPatient: Hmm... no. No, honestly, never. I really wish I could say yes, because if I could just somehow trick my brain into getting completely absorbed in a stack of sophomore essays on the causes of World War I—which, by the way, is a historical topic I actually love, but reading fifty teenagers write the exact same slightly-garbled paragraph about the assassination of Archduke Franz Ferdinand gets old incredibly fast—but if I could get lost in that, my life would be infinitely easier. \n\nBut no, that kind of deep, lose-track-of-time focus? That is strictly, one-hundred-percent reserved for things I actually want to be doing. When it comes to the mundane stuff, or the required paperwork, it's the exact opposite of getting absorbed. It's like... it's like my brain is actively repelling the information. \n\nTake those district curriculum documents you just mentioned. Just last month, the administration sent out this massive, eighty-page PDF about the new standardized testing rubrics for the social studies department. Just dry as dust, the most bureaucratic language you can possibly imagine. I sat down on a Sunday afternoon, told myself I was going to power through it so I wouldn't have to worry about it during the week. I had the whole afternoon blocked off, I had my coffee, the house was quiet because my wife was out running errands. And I sat there for three hours, and I swear to you, I maybe read four pages. \n\nThe whole time, I was just agonizing over every single sentence, reading the exact same paragraph about \"competency-based metrics\" over and over again until the words just started looking like random shapes on the screen. There was absolutely no flow, no losing track of time in a good way—just me staring at the clock on my laptop, feeling like every single minute was an hour, and getting absolutely nowhere. I ended up organizing my entire spice rack alphabetically just to escape it. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, if it's not a great sci-fi novel or a lesson I'm absolutely thrilled to teach, that deep focus just does not exist for me at all. It's like pulling teeth. I have never once in my entire career looked up from grading a stack of papers and thought, \"Wow, where did the time go?\" It's always more like, \"How on earth has it only been twelve minutes?\"\n\nDoctor: Given everything you've described—the lifelong attention struggles, the difficulty sustaining focus on non-preferred tasks, and how much this is affecting your work and your marriage—have you ever actually been referred for a formal ADHD evaluation, or has that just never come up in any conversations with doctors?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, it’s never... I’ve never been referred for anything like that. It just never came up in any doctor's office, ever. Which, looking back, is kind of wild, but I guess when I was a kid in the nineties, you know, the kids who got evaluated for that sort of thing were the ones bouncing off the walls, throwing chairs, or disrupting the whole class. I was never like that. I was the quiet kid in the back row staring out the window at the clouds, so my teachers just wrote on my report cards that I had so much potential if I would just apply myself, or that I needed to focus more. My parents just thought I was disorganized and maybe a little lazy, to be perfectly honest. So a doctor was never involved back then. \n\nAnd then as an adult... well, you know how it is, you go to your primary care guy for a sinus infection or to get a physical for life insurance, and you get maybe seven minutes with them. They check your blood pressure, ask if you smoke, and that’s it. I am not sitting there in the crinkly paper gown thinking about telling him that I spent three hours alphabetically organizing my spice rack instead of reading a district PDF, or asking if there is something wrong with my brain. It just doesn't feel like the right venue for that kind of deep conversation, especially when there are three other people coughing in the waiting room. So I never brought it up, and they obviously never asked. \n\nIt really wasn't until my wife... I mean, Sarah has been incredibly patient with me, she really has, but a couple months ago we had this massive argument because I completely forgot to pay the property taxes. I had the bill right on my desk, literally right next to my coffee mug, but I got distracted by grading those exact same sophomore essays we were just talking about, and the bill just vanished from my reality. She was so frustrated, and she finally just told me flat out that I needed to talk to a professional about my attention, because living like this isn't normal and she can't keep being my manager on top of her own job. That was really the wake-up call for me to seek out someone like you. But before making this specific appointment? No, no medical professional has ever mentioned an evaluation to me. Not once.\n\nDoctor: Based on everything you've described—the lifelong attention difficulties, the impact on your work and marriage, and the fact that you've never been formally evaluated—would you be open to getting a comprehensive ADHD evaluation now, either through me or through a specialist who can do the full testing?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I mean... yes, one hundred percent. That is exactly why I'm here. Honestly, just hearing you say that we can actually do that—that there's a formal process to figure this out—it's... it's a massive relief. Because for my entire life, I really just thought I was fundamentally flawed, you know? Like I was just missing some basic adulting gene that everyone else seemed to get. \n\nI’d look at the other teachers in my department, and they’d just sit in the staff room during their prep period, open their laptops, and just... work. They just quietly grade papers or update their grade books for forty-five minutes straight. And I’m sitting there, physically unable to start, organizing my dry-erase markers by color and thinking, \"Why is this so easy for them and so agonizing for me?\" I’ve spent so many years just quietly beating myself up about it, thinking I just needed to try harder or buy a better planner. \n\nSo, yes, I am completely open to an evaluation. Whether we do it right here with you, or if you need to send me to a specialist across town, I will go wherever you tell me to go. I don't really know what the full testing actually entails—like, I don't know if it's a massive written questionnaire, or if I have to do puzzles, or if it's just more talking like we're doing now, or what—but I don't even care. I'm ready. \n\nBecause like I said, Sarah is at her wit's end, and frankly, so am I. I can't keep white-knuckling my way through every single week, dreading Sunday afternoons because I know I have to fight my own brain just to prep for Monday morning. If we can figure out exactly what's going on up there, and if there's a way to actually manage it so I can just... function like a normal person and be a reliable husband... yeah. Please. Whatever the next step is, I am completely on board. Just tell me what I need to do."
  }
]
